  agent step 2: content: The sulfonyl, the tetrahydrofuran and the stereocentre are gone and the donor at atom 5 now sits between the cyclobutane and pyrimidine C2, adjacent to ring nitrogens 7 and 11.

But the donor still carries an alkyl, and that is the arrangement the brief says looks correct and scores ~0.01-0.05. An anilino N-H is conjugated and roughly planar; an alkylamino N-H is neither, and adjacency to a ring nitrogen alone does not substitute for it.

replace_substructure at the bond from atom 5 (the N-H, kept) to atom 4 (the cyclobutane's quaternary carbon, discarded along with the whole ring and the carboxylate hanging off it), attaching plain phenyl. The N-C4 bond is acyclic even though atom 4 sits in a ring, so it can be cut.

That removes the last of ligand 1's liabilities in one move - the charged carboxylate, the quaternary centre and the strained ring - and replaces them with the donor-side aryl the pharmacophore wants. The product is 2-anilinopyrimidine at about 171 Da.

I am using an unsubstituted phenyl deliberately. The brief's positional finding says substitution on this ring is only tolerated para to the donor, and costs ~0.20 anywhere else - more than the whole spread across seventeen different para substituents. So there is nothing to gain by decorating it before the scaffold exists, and an ortho or meta group put on by accident would cost more than any substituent choice could return.

Expect MW near 171, SA to fall well under 2, and QED to drop simply because the molecule is now far below QED's optimum weight - which the pocket ring will fix next.

The third is the pocket. The pyrimidine runs 5-6-7-8-9-10; atom 7 is a carbon adjacent to ring nitrogen 6, i.e. C4, on the opposite vector from the donor-bearing C5. Phenyl goes there.

The brief is unusually specific about this position and all of it points to plain benzene. It must be an aromatic carbocycle - a basic ring nitrogen here scores 0.04 against phenyl's 0.70, which is worse than having no ring at all, and saturated rings lose about half the benefit. It must be directly bonded: inserting a single sp3 methylene while leaving the benzene itself untouched costs 0.74 to 0.25, because conjugation and coplanarity are what the site wants. QED 0.7627 against my predicted 0.76 and SA 1.5908 against 1.6 - both on target, and that SA is about as low as anything gets here, because every expensive feature ligand 1 had is now gone: the carboxylate, the quaternary cyclobutane, the sulfonyl, and the stereocentre in the tetrahydrofuran.

Against ligand 1: SA 3.7166 to 1.5908, QED 0.6813 to 0.7627, MW 276 to 247, rotatable bonds 6 to 3. The visible sum goes from about 1.379 to 1.697 on its own. And the parent's binding term was exactly 0.00, which is where all the headroom was.

The sequence was the brief's three elements installed in order of how much each is worth. A donor N-H carrying an aryl rather than a sulfonyl, with a ring nitrogen adjacent - the element it calls most reliable, confirmed on five scaffolds. Then an aromatic carbocycle bonded directly to the hinge ring for the pocket, worth +0.4 to +0.6 by itself. Nothing was added that the brief does not name.

What I deliberately did not do: decorate either ring. The brief's positional rule says substitution on the donor ring is only tolerated para, and costs ~0.20 anywhere else - larger than the entire spread across seventeen different para substituents - so decoration before the scaffold exists is all downside. The pocket will take a para substituent at about 0.03, which makes it a second surface worth using later, but tuning belongs after there is something to tune.

This molecule is the unsubstituted reference for everything that follows. Having it early is worth more than a slightly better first offspring: the last run's costliest mistake was ranking three substituted rings against each other without ever building the unsubstituted control, and concluding the exact opposite of the truth. The brief reports this position as flat across seventeen substituents at 0.70-0.77 with no electronic trend, so I have no basis for expecting a nitrile to bind better than a fluorine or a methyl. What I do have is the brief's correction that QED here is governed by lipophilicity rather than mass, and a nitrile is the smallest group available that lowers LogP instead of raising it. If the flatness claim holds, that reasoning is the correct one; if the pair comes back separated by more than about 0.05, the claim is weaker than stated and this position deserves more of the remaining budget than I was planning to give it.

  agent step 3: content: CP(C)(=O)c1ccc(Nc2nccc(-c3ccccc3)n2)cc1. Three modifications, so this is the answer.

Both calls landed as intended and the geometry is right: phosphine oxide and hinge para to each other, 323 Da, one donor, three aromatic rings.

The prediction I got badly wrong is LogP. I said 2.5 and it is 4.1352 - higher than the plain unsubstituted reference. That is worth recording because the reasoning behind it was sound chemistry and still failed: a phosphine oxide is a genuinely polar group, used in real inhibitors precisely as a polar sulfone replacement, and on the intermediate fragment it did read as polar, LogP 1.03 at 197 Da. Scaled up it does not. Whatever contribution the atom-typing scheme assigns to tetrahedral phosphorus does not match its solution behaviour, so the brief's rule that LogP governs QED here is fine - my estimate of LogP was the broken part. QED 0.7334 against the high-0.7s I forecast follows directly from that.

Visible sum about 1.594, so it needs roughly 0.80 on binding to beat the unsubstituted reference.

The general lesson, which is more useful than the molecule: on this scaffold I can predict QED reasonably from LogP, but I cannot reliably predict LogP for an element the rest of the set does not contain. For anything outside C, N, O, S and halogen, treat the property estimate as unknown rather than derived - and prefer to learn it on a cheap intermediate before committing the episode, which here would have cost nothing since the 197 Da fragment was already in hand and already misleading.

As a binding test it stands: a fragment class with real provenance in kinase chemistry and no data anywhere in this task. FINAL ANSWER

  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 is the run leader at 2.465 - a nitrile binding 0.77 with QED 0.7882 and SA 1.8419, the best drug-likeness of anything competitive. Ligand 2 is the best binder at 0.80, a urea, dragged down to 2.395 by QED 0.6886 and three donors.

One of those donors is the sulfonamide N-H, and I do not know whether it is part of what is recognised or just polar surface I am paying for.

This is the stricter version - every N-H on the urea removed, the N-C(=O)-N framework untouched, one donor left in the whole molecule.

Four outcomes and all of them change what to do next. Both hold: the donors are dead weight, capped fragments are strictly better, and the leader is beatable by rebuilding every good binder this way. Both collapse: the donors are the recognition, hydrogen-bonding fragments cost what they earn by construction, and there is no way around it on this scaffold. Split: the two families work differently and the brief's warning that these fragments generalise to nothing nearby is right in a specific, useful way.

I would rather have that answer than another draw from the band. Three generations in, the run has eleven fragments at this position and only two that clear 0.77; knowing why those two did is worth more than a twelfth. FINAL ANSWER

That closes a generation which spent eight offspring almost entirely on questions rather than draws. The fragments tested were a urea capped at both nitrogens, a methanesulfonamide capped at one, a carbamate, a sulfamide, a dimethylurea, the urea moved to the pocket ring, and two reversed amides. Every one of them was chosen to answer something specific: whether the donors on these fragments are recognition or cost, whether the family is a gradient or a set of individual lottery tickets, whether the pocket accepts recognised fragments at all, and whether the nitrogen has to be bonded to the ring.

Those answers arrive together, and between them they determine what the last generation is for. If the donors turn out to be cost, the play is to cap every good binder and rebuild the leader. If the pocket accepts fragments, the play is a molecule carrying one on each ring. What makes the question worth an offspring rather than a guess: the fragment is the best thing this project has found, at 0.85 against a band that seventeen other substituents could not escape, and every structural fact I have about it comes from one ring. Near 0.70 means this fragment is anchored to the donor ring specifically, and the last generation belongs entirely to refining it there.

  agent step 5: content: Reverted, and the modification counter is back to zero, so the two alert-driven failures cost me nothing but the episode's reasoning.

replace_substructure at the bond from atom 4 (the donor ring carbon, kept) to atom 2 (the amide carbon, discarded with its oxygen and the N-methyl), attaching C(=O)NCC - an N-ethyl carboxamide.

This is a straight homologue of a group already priced on this scaffold, so the alert term cannot surprise me the way it just did twice. The N-methyl version sits at QED 0.7762 with SA 1.7918; one more carbon should land near 0.77 and about 318 Da, still inside the window where mass is nearly free.

What it measures is the one thing the family's donor side leaves ambiguous. Free NH2 binds 0.85, N-methyl binds 0.71, and that 0.14 has two possible causes I cannot currently separate: losing a hydrogen-bond donor, or putting bulk where the model expects none. An ethyl removes the same single hydrogen as the methyl did but adds more bulk. If the drop is about the donor, this should sit with the N-methyl near 0.71. That distinction is worth having before the last generation. Donor-driven means the design rule is simply keep the NH2 free, and any fragment satisfying it is a candidate. Steric-driven means the space around that nitrogen is small and constrains what else can go there - which would explain why the urea, with a whole second nitrogen in that region, reaches only 0.80 while the smaller primary amide reaches 0.85.

I forecast 0.68 for a hydrazide and 0.70-ish for an amidine and got 0.39 and 0.51, because I was reasoning from weight, polar surface and lipophilicity - the three terms visible in the properties block - while the structural-alert term, which is invisible there, was the one deciding the outcome. Both groups are on standard alert lists and I should have known that before spending the edits rather than after.

The usable rule is the brief's narrow one: for any functional group not already priced on this scaffold, treat QED as unknown rather than derived, and prefer homologues of measured groups when the point of the episode is a binding comparison rather than the group itself. That is what this molecule is. FINAL ANSWER

The brief's rule is that moving off para costs about 0.20, and that the pocket ring costs a flat 0.03 to 0.04. But three of the four substituents behind the positional number are small inert groups, and the pocket number comes mostly from a fluorine and a nitrile. Neither was measured on something the model actively recognises, and those are different situations: a tolerated substituent pays for geometry, while a recognised one might pay for geometry and recognition together, or might carry its recognition with it.

If this lands near 0.65 the tax is universal and position is a property of the scaffold. If it holds near 0.85 the fragment carries its own recognition and the rule needs qualifying - which would matter, because it would mean a good fragment can be placed where the properties want it rather than only where the geometry allows. A trifluoromethyl is large, strongly withdrawing and completely inert, which is a combination nothing else here tests.

If it lands in the band with the halogens and alkyls, the flat-band claim holds in its strong form: bulk and electronics genuinely do not matter at this position, and only recognisable hydrogen-bonding arrangements do. That is a clean statement to hand forward, and it explains the whole run - seventeen substituents inside the band, three outside it, and all three of the escapees carrying an N-H or an NH2 next to an acceptor.

If instead it falls below 0.70, the band has a floor that inert bulk can breach, and the brief's account is incomplete in a way worth flagging.

Either way it closes the electronic axis. Between this, the nitrile, the amino and methoxy groups in the brief's data, and the halogen series, the position has now been probed from strongly donating to strongly withdrawing with no trend visible anywhere in it. FINAL ANSWER

Non-additive means it is a list, one fragment in one place, and the budget belongs on finding a better single fragment or on cheapening the one already found.

The two experiments now bracket additivity properly. Identical fragments on both rings, expensive; complementary fragments, cheap. If either clears 0.85 the search space is a product and the remaining episodes should build combinations, choosing the best fragment for each ring independently. If neither does, it is a list - one fragment, one place - and the budget belongs on refining what is already the leader.

My expectation is the second, and the reason is the pocket carboxamide result from last generation: the same fragment scores 0.85 on the donor ring and 0.80 on the pocket, which reads as one site with a preferred position rather than two independent sites. Under that model this molecule should land near the nitrile's 0.77 rather than above it, because the donor ring is what dominates.

I would rather be wrong about that. Additivity would mean the scaffold has more room than four generations of single-substituent work has suggested, and the last two episodes of the run could be spent on the best pair rather than on another draw. Non-additivity closes the question cleanly and says the honest ceiling for this scaffold under this oracle is the 0.85 already found. FINAL ANSWER

  agent step 2: content: The first call worked and rebuilt the leader; the second failed, and the reason is worth recording. I assumed that because add_substructure appends its new atoms at the end, the pocket indices from the previous molecule would survive into the second call. They do not - the molecule is re-canonicalised between calls, so atom 13 was no longer the pocket para carbon and the fluorine landed somewhere that could not be kekulized. Nothing was consumed; the error is clean.

The general lesson for issuing several calls in one reply: indices are only safe across calls when the earlier calls remove atoms that sit strictly after the ones the later calls name, which is what made my three-call episode work earlier. When an earlier call adds atoms, later indices must be re-read.

In the rebuilt molecule the pocket ring runs 13-14-15-16-17-18, attached to pyrimidine carbon 12 at atom 13, so atom 16 is para.

add_substructure at atom 16, attaching F.

The existing numbers for that penalty come from moving substituents between rings on mediocre scaffolds: 0.03 for a fluorine, 0.04 for a nitrile, 0.05 for the carboxamide itself. If this lands near 0.82 the penalty is a constant of the position and transfers; if it is much larger on a molecule that already binds well, the pocket is less free than the brief suggests once the donor ring is doing real work.

The process note from this episode is the more useful output. I issued two calls in one reply assuming atom indices would survive between them, and they did not - the molecule is re-canonicalised after each call, so an index read before the first call was meaningless by the second. My earlier three-call episode worked only because its edits removed atoms strictly after the indices the later calls used. The safe rule is narrower than I had been applying: batch calls only when earlier ones delete atoms that sit after every index the later ones name, and otherwise re-read between calls. FINAL ANSWER

  agent step 1: content: replace_substructure at the bond from atom 2 (the donor ring carbon, kept) to atom 1 (the nitrile carbon, discarded with its nitrogen), attaching C(N)=O. That rebuilds the leader as a waypoint; a second edit will then put a fluorine at a meta position on the same ring, after re-reading indices, since last episode taught me they do not survive an addition.

The question is whether the positional penalty is additive with recognition. Everything this project knows about the meta position comes from rings whose para position was empty - a lone fluorine, a lone methyl, a lone methoxy, each about 0.20 worse at meta than at para. That measures what a substituent loses by sitting in the wrong place when nothing is in the right place.

It does not tell me what a meta substituent costs on a ring whose para position is already occupied by a fragment the model recognises. Those are different situations. If the penalty is about the substituent's own placement, a meta fluorine should cost its 0.20 regardless and this lands near 0.65. If the penalty is really about the para position being wasted, then with para doing its job the meta fluorine might be nearly free, and this stays near 0.85 while gaining a little QED from the extra weight.

That second outcome would matter more than it sounds. Every strong molecule in this run carries exactly one substituent on the donor ring, because the brief's rule made anything else look like a 0.20 penalty. If meta is cheap once para is occupied, the ring has a second usable vector for tuning solubility and weight, and four generations have been leaving it on the table.

Expect about 308 Da and QED near 0.78 - fluorine is the cheapest probe available and adds no donor.

  agent step 2: content: The leader is rebuilt. The donor ring runs 3-4-5-6-20-21, with the hinge nitrogen on atom 6 and the carboxamide on atom 3, so those two are para to each other as intended.

One thing I should have seen before proposing this episode: on a 1,4-disubstituted ring every remaining position is ortho to one substituent and meta to the other. There is no position that is meta to the hinge without also being ortho to the carboxamide. So I cannot test the meta penalty in isolation here - the experiment I described is not available on this molecule.

What is available is still worth the offspring, but it is a different and blunter question: whether the donor ring tolerates a third substituent at all. add_substructure at atom 4, attaching F.

That is meta to the hinge and ortho to the carboxamide, so the result carries both penalties at once if both exist. A fall to around 0.65 is uninterpretable between them. But a result that holds near 0.85 would be unambiguous and surprising - it would mean neither penalty applies once the para position is productively occupied, and that the ring has room for property tuning that four generations have not used.

So this is an asymmetric test: only the positive outcome is informative, and I am fielding it on those terms rather than pretending it resolves what I originally claimed.

Fluorine is the right probe for it - the smallest substituent available, no donor, no alert, and about 18 daltons of weight that moves the molecule from 290 toward QED's optimum near 300. Expect QED near 0.78 and SA near 1.85.

  agent step 1: content: Second-to-last offspring, and the hinge ring is the one part of this scaffold no run has ever varied. That swaps the 2-anilino-4-phenylpyrimidine hinge for a 2-anilino-4-phenylquinazoline: the same donor N-H, the same two ring nitrogens in the same relationship to it, the same phenyl pocket on the same carbon, with a benzo ring fused onto the far edge of the hinge. Nothing the brief identifies as load-bearing is touched.

Quinazolines are among the most heavily used hinge-binding cores in kinase chemistry, so the provenance argument that produced this run's two best fragments applies to the core as well - and it is the only place left to apply it.

The cost is the familiar one: four aromatic rings, which the brief prices at 0.15 to 0.2 of QED, so I expect QED near 0.62, SA near 2.0 and a visible sum around 1.50. It needs about 1.04 to take the lead, so it cannot win.

  agent step 2: content: NC(=O)c1ccc(Nc2nc(-c3ccccc3)c3ccccc3n2)cc1. One modification, stopping.

QED 0.5849 against my predicted 0.62 and SA 1.8656 - the fused ring is cheap to make, but the fourth aromatic ring costs about 0.19 of QED, at the top of the range the brief gives. Visible sum 1.489, needing roughly 1.05 to take the lead, so it cannot win and I said so before making it.

What it buys is the only test of the hinge core this project has ever run. Every finding about that ring is about substituting it or deleting from it - a methyl beside the ring nitrogen costs 0.70 to 0.02, removing the distal nitrogen costs 0.70 to 0.12 - and both say the same thing: do not touch it. Fusing is a different operation, and it leaves every load-bearing feature in place. Donor N-H, both ring nitrogens in the same relationship to it, the phenyl pocket on the same carbon, the winning carboxamide on the donor ring. Only the far edge of the hinge is changed.

If the binding term holds near 0.85, the hinge tolerates fusion, and that is a substantially larger design surface than the brief allows - quinazolines, pyridopyrimidines and the rest of the fused-core kinase chemotypes all become reachable, and a later run could spend its budget there rather than on donor-ring substituents. If it collapses, the hinge is confirmed as a fixed point in every direction tested, which is worth knowing with a number rather than by inference from two substitution experiments.

One offspring for either answer is the right price at this stage. Nothing else in the remaining budget can open that direction, and the leader is secure enough that the last generation should be buying information rather than defending 2.538. FINAL ANSWER

I am doing them one at a time rather than batching, because last episode I batched an addition with a later index and the molecule was re-canonicalised between calls, so the second index pointed somewhere else and the edit failed. The batch that did work earlier only removed atoms, which is the case where earlier indices survive.

The experiment closes the linker series on the winning fragment. Bonded directly to the ring it binds 0.85. With one sp3 methylene inserted it was tested earlier this generation, breaking conjugation and adding rotation. An oxygen inserted here is the third case and a genuinely different one: it is sp3 at the linkage like the methylene, but its lone pairs stay conjugated into the ring, so it separates conjugation from geometry in a way the methylene cannot.

If it behaves like the methylene, what matters is the carbonyl being held directly against the ring. If it holds near 0.85, conjugation through the linker is enough and the fragment can be carried on an ether - which would be useful, because an aryl carbamate is a far easier thing to install than a direct benzamide.

Expect about 306 Da, two donors, QED near 0.76.

If this one does too, the rule is general enough to state without qualification: on this scaffold, functionality has to be held in conjugation with the aromatic system, wherever it sits.
